left, its south-arrival endpoint must be `SR`. That is
a third west departure on level nine, a contradiction.

Both level-seven continuations are excluded. No later wake
choices or period bounds beyond `N>=6` were used. The
clock comparison excluding P is in the lower orientation;
the whole band exclusion is not asserted for an upper cap.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-adjacent-wakes-force-six-2026-09-13-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_adjacent_wakes_force_six_2026-09-13

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_adjacent_wakes_force_six_2026-09-13.md`.

### Wakes at lower levels four and five force a wake at six

For a lower `(1,2,2)` cap with drift `(1,-3)`,

```text
omega_4=omega_5=1  implies  omega_6=1.
```

The proof excludes the four-visit word on level six, independently
of every later wake indicator and of the period. It removes one of
the five bands in `odd_skew_fat122_five_lower_bands_2026-09-13.md`.
The four remaining lower bands do not yet exclude the cap profile
or the drift orbit.

#### Suppose level six is non-wake

The lower-band normal form fixes

```text
0: WR
1: NR,SL
2: WR,WL
3: SR,SL,NR,SL
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,WR
5: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL,NR
6: ER,EL,ER,WL.
```

Let `Wi` be the west departures on level seven. They feed the
level-four west arrivals `WR,WL,WL,WR,WR`. There is only one north
arrival on level seven, supplied by `6:EL`. The twelve-step return
from `W2` through the lower cap ends there, forcing the consecutive
pair `W2=SR`, `W3=NL`. Its other west departures are all `SR`.
Thus, for `f=omega_7`,

```text
7: SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,SR,[SL]^(1-f).
```

Number the east arrivals on level eight as `E1,...,E5`. Their
sources on level five are `SL1,NR1,NR2,SL2,NR3`, in that order.
The fixed paths from `W1,W4,W5` end at `E2,E3,E5`:

```text
W1 -> 4:first WR -> 5:first NR -> E2;
W4 -> 4:second WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL
   -> 4:EL -> 5:second NR -> E3;
W5 -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR -> E5.
```

Each endpoint is the physical cell immediately north of its
starting level-seven cell. As in the preceding normal-form proof,
these paths force `E3=ER`: if it were `EL`, its eight-step return
from the south predecessor of `W4` would force `E2=ER`, and the
path from `W1` would close a four-step right-to-right return to
`W4`. Hence `E3=ER` is the fifth south departure on level eight,
feeding the `SL` immediately after `W4`.

Level nine has exactly one west departure, denoted `D`, because
the only west arrival on level six is its final `WL`. We now use
this fact to exclude both turns at `E5`.

#### If the last east arrival is `EL`

Suppose `E5=EL`. The south predecessor of `W5=SR` has a four-step
first same-cell return to `E5`. Its source must be `E4=ER`, and
`E4,E5` are consecutive stable events on level eight.

The sole west departure `D` has the eight-step first return

```text
D -> 6:WL -> 5:SL2 -> E4=ER -> 7:W5=SR
  -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR -> E5=EL -> north arrival on 9.
```

If `D=SR`, its consecutive target would be `NL`, producing a
second west departure on level nine. Thus `D=NL`, with consecutive
target `NR`. Let `X` be its incoming predecessor on level eight.
It is the north departure immediately preceding `E5=EL` in north
departure order. Since `E4=ER,E5=EL` are consecutive stable events,
`X` lies before `E4`.

But the shorter path

```text
8:X -> 9:D=NL -> 6:WL -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=ER
```

is a four-step first same-cell return. Its source must be `EL`,
immediately preceding `E4` in stable order. It would therefore be
the third east arrival `E3=EL`, contrary to `E3=ER`.

The argument uses the first return to `E4`, not the longer path
through `E5`: the latter also visits the same level-eight cell at
`E4` and is not a first return.

#### If the last east arrival is `ER`

Suppose instead `E5=ER`. Its south step completes a four-step
return from `W5=SR`, forcing a following `SL`. Thus `f=0`, and
the south arrivals on level seven are exactly

```text
SR,SL,SR,SR,SL,SR,SL.
```

Consequently `E3=ER` is the fifth south departure on level eight,
and `E5=ER` is the seventh. The sixth south departure, denoted `J`,
feeds `W5=SR`. Its four-step first return to `E5=ER` makes
`J=WL`, immediately followed by `E5` in stable order.

The intervening east arrival `E4` cannot be `ER`. A left turn
between the consecutive east arrivals `E3=ER` and `E4=ER` would
have to be `WL`, contributing an additional south departure before
`E4`. There would then be no room for `E4` and the later `E5` in
the remaining south-departure positions six and seven. Therefore
`E4=EL`.

In fact `E3=ER,E4=EL` are consecutive stable events. A `WL` between
them would be the only remaining south departure `J`, whose next
stable event is already `E5`, not `E4`. An intervening `WR` would
itself require such a left turn. No other east arrival is available
between `E3` and `E4`.

Now `D` has the four-step first return

```text
D -> 6:WL -> 5:SL2 -> E4=EL -> north arrival on 9.
```

Again `D=SR` would create a second west departure at its `NL`
target. Hence `D=NL`, with target `NR`. Its incoming predecessor
`X` on level eight is the north departure immediately preceding
`E4`. The path

```text
8:X -> 9:D=NL -> 6:WL -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=EL
```

is a four-step first same-cell return. It requires `X=WR` to be
the immediately preceding stable event. That event is instead
`E3=ER`, a south departure. This is the final contradiction.

Both turns at `E5` are impossible. The assumption `omega_6=0`
is therefore false. The return contradictions after the initial
band use only zero displacement; the lower-cap restriction enters
through the five-band normal form.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-early-wake-2026-09-12-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_early_wake_2026-09-12

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_early_wake_2026-09-12.md`.

### A lower `(1,2,2)` cap requires a wake on level four or five

For drift `(1,-3)`, every lower cap of profile `(1,2,2)` satisfies

```text
omega_4 + omega_5 >= 1.
```

This is an all-period consequence of stable chronology. It does not assume
growth eight, an upper cap profile, or a bound on any inward multiplicity.
The conclusion supplies an additional wake near this cap; it does not by
itself exclude the cap or the drift orbit.

Use transverse coordinate `t=3x+y` and representatives `(0,t)`. An edge of
voltage `lambda` changes stable lifted time by `1-N*lambda`, with voltages
`1,-1,0,0` for `E,W,N,S`. Stable times increase within each class. Any
period with this drift has `N>=6`, since the step axes alternate and the
vertical displacement has magnitude three.

#### Suppose levels four and five are non-wake

The cap normal form gives

```text
0: WR
1: NR,SL
2: WR,WL
3: SR,SL,NR,SL
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL
5 A: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL
  B: NR,SL,SR,NL,NR,SL.
```

Let `e=omega_6`. The counts on level six are

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(r,3-r,6+e-3r,3-r),       r=0,1,2.
```

The four-step return from the south predecessor of `5:SR` through
`5:SR,2:WR,3:NR` to the second east arrival on level six excludes `r=0`.
The same return and stable order leave the following possibilities:

| Name | Level five | Level six, followed by `[WR]^e` |
|---|---|---|
| `U1` | A | `ER,EL,WR,EL,WR,WL,WR,WL` |
| `U2` | A | `ER,EL,WR,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL` |
| `U3` | B | `WR,WL,ER,EL,WR,EL,WR,WL` |
| `U4` | A | `WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,EL,WR,WL` |
| `L` | A | `ER,EL,ER,WL` |

For completeness, the final-`WL` restriction used here is independent of
`e`. Put `V=tau(2:WL)` and `Z=tau(4:second WL)`. Edge matching gives
`tau(4:last WR)=V-N-2`, so `Z<=V-N-6`. The third east arrival on level
six has time `Z+2-N<=V-2N-4`, whereas its last south departure feeds the
last `SL` on level five, after the north arrival at time `V-N+3`.
Thus the last south departure on level six is after its last east arrival
and must be `WL`. Its final stable port is consequently `WL` when `e=0`
and a following `WR` when `e=1`. Removing that optional final `WR`
reduces the word classification to the one in
`odd_skew_fat122_even_band_2026-09-12.md`.

The branch `L,e=0` was excluded in that note. We first exclude all four
`U` branches for either value of `e`.

#### Two adjacent west departures remove `U1` and `U2`

Let `W1,...,W4` be the west departures on level seven. They feed the
ordered west arrivals `WR,WL,WL,WR` on level four. Write `Ni` for its
north arrivals in order. Exact returns force the consecutive stable pairs

| Branch | First return | Second return |
|---|---|---|
| `U1` | `W2 -> N1` | `W3 -> N3` |
| `U2` | `W2 -> N1` | `W3 -> N4` |
| `U3` | `W2 -> N2` | `W3 -> N4` |
| `U4` | `W2 -> N2` | `W3 -> N4` |

The first return has length twelve for `U1,U2,U3`, and length eight for
`U4`. It starts by following

```text
W2 -> 4:first WL -> 3:SR -> 0:WR -> 1:NR -> 4:ER
   -> 3:first SL -> first east arrival on 6.
```

For `U1,U2,U3`, this east arrival is `ER`; continue through
`5:SR,2:WR,3:NR` to the second east arrival on level six, which is `EL`.
For `U4`, the first east arrival itself is `EL`. Its north step gives
the target in the table. The second return is always the four-step path

```text
W3 -> 4:second WL -> 3:last SL -> 6:last EL -> target north arrival.
```

All these returns have zero displacement and no intermediate level-seven
visit. The extra final `WR` when `e=1` does not alter their north ordinals.

In `U1` and `U2`, the source of the first return cannot be `NL`: it
would be a north arrival before `N1`. Thus it is `SR`, followed immediately
by `N1=NL`. This target is the next west departure, namely `W3`. The
return starting there must then end at the next north arrival `N2`.
Its stated target is instead `N3` or `N4`, a contradiction.

#### The remaining two `U` branches are impossible

In `U3,U4`, if `W2=SR`, its consecutive target `N2=NL` would again
be `W3`. The second return would require the next north arrival to be
`N4`, skipping `N3`. Therefore `W2=NL`, and consecutive order makes it
`N1`, followed by `N2=NR`.

For `U3`, this gives the eight-step return

```text
6:first WR -> 7:W2 -> 4:first WL -> 3:SR -> 0:WR
           -> 1:NR -> 4:ER -> 3:first SL -> 6:first ER.
```

It has no intermediate level-six visit. Both endpoints turn right, so
`U3` is impossible.

For `U4`, suppose `W3=NL`. Its consecutive target `N4=NR` makes this
source `N3`, fed by the second `WR` on level six. Then

```text
6:second WR -> 7:W3 -> 4:second WL -> 3:last SL -> 6:last EL
```

is a four-step return skipping the intervening `WL,ER` in the level-six
word. Thus `W3=SR`, followed immediately by `N4=NL=W4`. Since `W2`
is the first north arrival, `W1=SR`. These facts force

```text
7: SR,NL,NR,SL,NR,SL,SR,NL,NR,(SL,NR)^e,[SL]^(1-f),
   f=omega_7.
```

Let `E1,...,E4` be the east arrivals on level eight, supplied by
`SL1,NR1,NR2,SL2` on level five. The path from `7:W4=NL` through
`4:last WR,5:NL,2:WL,1:SL,4:EL,5:last NR` ends at `E3`.
If `E3=ER`, its south step would complete an eight-step return to a
south arrival on level seven. But the next stable event after this `NL`
is `NR`. Hence `E3=EL`.

Now take the level-eight south departure feeding `7:W3=SR`. Its path
first follows the four-step return from `W3` to `W4`, then follows the
preceding chain to `E3`. This is a twelve-step return to level eight
with no intermediate visit there. The opposite-turn source must be
`ER`, and consecutive order identifies it as `E2`. But

```text
7:W1=SR -> 4:first WR -> 5:first NR -> E2 -> 7:W3=SR
```

is now a four-step return with two right-turn endpoints. This excludes
`U4`, for either parity on levels six and seven.

#### The forced wake on level six

Only branch `L,e=1` remains:

```text
5: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL
6: ER,EL,ER,WL,WR.
```

The twelve-step return from `7:W2` to its first north arrival makes
`W2=SR`, immediately followed by `NL=W3`. There are now two north
arrivals on level seven. Its count equations and R-first order give
exactly the following two words, where `f=omega_7`:

```text
P: SR,SL,SR,NL,NR,SL,SR,[SL]^(1-f),
Q: SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-f).
```

Indeed, two `NL` ports would leave only two `SR` ports and no `SL`
(and would require `f=0`), which cannot put two west departures before
the first north arrival in an alternating word. There is therefore one
`NL`, three `SR`, and one `NR`. Two `SR` precede the `NL`; the final
`SR` and the `NR` may occur in either order.

Write the three `SR` ports as `SR1,SR2,SR3`. As in the earlier even-band
argument, two fixed paths are

```text
7:SR1 -> 4:first WR -> 5:first NR -> 8:E2,
7:SR3 -> 4:last WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL
      -> 4:EL -> 5:last NR -> 8:E3.
```

If `E3=EL`, its south-departure predecessor would have to be `E2=ER`,
and the first path would return from `SR1` to `SR3` with two right turns.
Thus `E3=ER`, and its south step must return to `SL` immediately after
`SR3`. This removes `P,f=1`.

Let `J` be the level-eight south departure feeding `SR3`. Its eight-step
return to `E3=ER` makes `J=WL`, with `WL,ER` consecutive there.

#### The last east arrival and its preceding north departure

For `P`, now with `f=0`, the south arrivals on level seven are
`SR1,SL1,SR2,SL2,SR3,SL3`. Thus `E3=ER` is the last south departure on
level eight, and `E4=EL` follows it immediately in stable order.

For `Q`, `E3=ER` feeds `SL2`. If `f=1`, that is again the last south
arrival and the same conclusion follows. If `f=0`, one last `SL` follows
the sole `NR` on level seven. This `NR` is fed by the final `WR` on
level six. In contrast, `E4` is fed by `6:WL -> 5:last SL`, so

```text
tau(E4)=tau(6:WL)+2-N < tau(6:last WR).
```

It cannot feed the last south arrival on level seven. Hence `E4=EL`,
and the final south departure on level eight is a `WL` after `E4`.
There is no available left turn between `E3=ER` and `E4=EL`: that final
`WL` occurs later, and no other east arrival lies between them. They are
again consecutive stable events.

In every remaining case, the block ending at `E4` is therefore

```text
Z=WR, J=WL, E3=ER, E4=EL,
```

where `Z` is the north departure immediately preceding `E4` in north
departure order. To see that this preceding right turn is `WR`, note that
`J` is the fifth south departure in `P` and the fourth in `Q`. The return
from the first south predecessor through `SR1` to `E2` makes `E2`
either `EL`, or the second south departure `ER`. Thus the right turn
before `J` cannot be either of the earlier east arrivals.

#### The inward returns force a forbidden translated path

There are exactly two west departures on level nine, feeding `6:WL,WR`.
Call them `T1,T2` in order, and let `Nq` be the north arrival fed by
`8:E4=EL`. The first has the four-step return

```text
T1 -> 6:WL -> 5:last SL -> 8:E4 -> Nq.
```

If `T1=NL`, its consecutive target is `NR`, so its incoming predecessor
is `8:Z`, the preceding north departure. The return

```text
8:Z -> 9:T1 -> 6:WL -> 5:last SL -> 8:E4
```

would then skip `J,E3` on level eight. Therefore `T1=SR`, and its
consecutive target is `NL=T2`. In particular there is no `SR` later
than `T2` in the level-nine word.

The path from `T2` through `6:last WR,7:NR` ends at an east arrival `F`
on level ten: it is the second east arrival for `P`, and the third for
`Q`. It must be `EL`. Otherwise its south step would return to a
south arrival immediately after `9:T2=NL`; that visit would have to be
`SR`, and the only `SR` on level nine occurs earlier.

The south departure on level ten feeding `9:T1=SR` now has an eight-step
return, through `T1`, the four-step return to `T2`, and the path to `F`.
Its opposite-turn source is `ER`, immediately preceding `F` in stable
order. Thus it is the immediately preceding east arrival `F'`.

For `P`, `F'` is supplied by `7:SL1`. For `Q`, it is supplied by
`7:SL2`. Let `Y` be the level-eight south departure feeding that `SL`.
In `P`, every south departure precedes `E4`; in `Q`, this particular
departure is `E3=ER`. Hence in either case `tau(Y)<tau(E4)`.

But the now-forced path is

```text
8:Y -> 7:SL -> 10:F'=ER -> 9:T1=SR -> 6:WL
    -> 5:last SL -> 8:E4=EL.
```

Its direction word is `SESWSE`, of length six and displacement `(1,-3)`.
Therefore

```text
tau(E4)=tau(Y)+6-N.
```

The strict stable inequality requires `N<6`, contradicting the drift's
kinematic lower bound. This removes the final branch and proves that
levels four and five cannot both be non-wake.

#### Remaining cap branches

The lower `(1,2,2)` problem now has three early wake patterns:
`(omega_4,omega_5)=(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)`. The upper cap is unrestricted in
this theorem. The final voltage inequality has the opposite sign in
upper inward coordinates, so this wake restriction is asserted only
for the lower cap in the stated drift orientation.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-even-band-2026-09-12-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_even_band_2026-09-12

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_even_band_2026-09-12.md`.

### The first even band at a lower `(1,2,2)` cap

Later result: [the lower early-wake theorem](05_Negative_skew.md#source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-early-wake-2026-09-12-md) excludes the quiet-four/five/six hypothesis studied below. The four local orders are intermediate necessary cases, not remaining highway candidates.

Consider a lower cap of profile `(1,2,2)` for drift `(1,-3)`. If levels four,
five, and six are all non-wake, the four-visit alternative on level six is
impossible. Level six must have eight visits, in one of four orders listed
below. The proof has no period, width, or inward multiplicity cutoff.

This closes a branch of the cap problem. The four surviving orders and cap
patterns having a wake on level four, five, or six remain unresolved.

Use `t=3x+y`, representatives `(0,t)`, and the port and lifted-time conventions
of `odd_skew_lower_fat_cap_normal_form_2026-09-12.md`. That argument gives

```text
0: WR
1: NR,SL
2: WR,WL
3: SR,SL,NR,SL
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL
5 A: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL
  B: NR,SL,SR,NL,NR,SL.
```

The two `SL` ports on level three are numbered in their stable order, and
the same convention applies to repeated ports on other levels.

#### The two level-six count rows

Level six has three east arrivals from level three and three south departures
feeding level five. Since its excess is zero, its possible count rows are

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL) = (r,3-r,6-3r,3-r),       r=0,1,2.
```

Let `E2` be its second east arrival and let `J` be the south departure feeding
the unique `5:SR`. Order-preserving edge matching gives the four-step return

```text
J -> 5:SR -> 2:WR -> 3:NR -> E2.
```

It has direction word `SWNE`. Its endpoints are consecutive visits to the
same physical level-six cell with opposite turns. This excludes `r=0`,
since neither endpoint can then supply a right turn.

#### Suppose level six has four visits

For `r=2`, the two alternating words are `ER,EL,ER,WL` and `ER,WL,ER,EL`.
The latter would make `J=WL` and `E2=ER`. The `WL` is the second south
departure, so level five would have to use word B. But the south step from
this `ER` completes a four-step return from `5:SR` to a south arrival on
level five, whereas the next stable event after `SR` in B is `NL`.
Thus the only possibility is

```text
5: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL
6: ER,EL,ER,WL.
```

Let `W1,...,W4` be the west departures on level seven in stable order.
They feed the west arrivals `WR,WL,WL,WR` on level four. Level seven has
one north arrival, fed by `6:EL`. Its second west departure has the forced
return

```text
W2 -> 4:first WL -> 3:SR -> 0:WR -> 1:NR -> 4:ER
   -> 3:first SL -> 6:first ER -> 5:SR -> 2:WR
   -> 3:NR -> 6:EL -> only north arrival on 7.
```

The direction word is `WSWNESESWNEN`. These twelve steps have zero
displacement and no intermediate level-seven visit. If the north arrival
were `NR`, all west departures would be `SR`, giving two right turns at
the endpoints. Therefore the north arrival is `NL`. The source is `SR`,
immediately followed by this `NL` in stable order. Since it is the second
west departure, the complete level-seven word is

```text
SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,[SL]^(1-f),       f=omega_7.
```

Write its right-turn south arrivals as `SR1,SR2,SR3`. In west-departure
order they are `W1,W2,W4`, with `NL=W3`.

#### Two returns through level eight

Level eight has four east arrivals `E1,...,E4`, supplied by the east
departures `SL1,NR1,NR2,SL2` on level five. Two fixed paths are

```text
7:SR1 -> 4:first WR -> 5:first NR -> E2,
7:SR3 -> 4:last WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL
      -> 4:EL -> 5:last NR -> E3.
```

They have lengths three and seven respectively and both end at the same
physical level-eight cell immediately north of the starting level-seven
cell.

The arrival `E3` must be `ER`. If it were `EL`, take the level-eight south
departure `D` feeding `7:SR3`. Its path through the second displayed chain
would return to `E3` in eight steps, with no intermediate level-eight
visit. The opposite-turn source would be `ER`, and consecutive stable
order makes it `E2`: choosing `E1` would skip the intervening east arrival
`E2`. The first displayed chain would then give a four-step return from
`7:SR1` to `7:SR3`, with right turns at both endpoints. This is impossible.

Thus `E3=ER`. Its south step completes an eight-step return from `7:SR3`
to a south arrival on the same level-seven cell. Hence `SR3` must have a
following stable event. This excludes `f=1` and forces

```text
7: SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL.
```

The level-eight predecessor `D` of `SR3` must now be `WL`, immediately
followed by `E3=ER`. The five south arrivals on level seven, in order, are
`SR1,SL1,SR2,SR3,SL2`. Therefore this `WL` is the fourth south departure
on level eight and `E3` is the fifth and last. In particular `E4=EL`.

The south departure feeding `SR1` has a four-step return to `E2`. If
`E2=EL`, its source is `ER` and must be `E1`. Since it is the first south
departure, stable order makes `E1,E2` the initial pair `ER,EL`.
If `E2=ER`, its source is `WL`, again the first south departure. Then
`E1` cannot be `ER`, so `E1=EL`, and alternation forces the prefix
`WR,EL,WR,WL,ER` through `E2`.

There are exactly two `ER` ports in either case. With `e=omega_8`, flow
gives the level-eight counts `(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(2,2,3+e,3)`. The fourth and
fifth south departures are the consecutive `WL,E3` pair, and the last east
arrival is `E4=EL`. The resulting words are exactly

```text
8 A: ER,EL,WR,WL,WR,WL,WR,WL,ER,EL,[WR]^e,
  B: WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,WL,ER,EL,[WR]^e.
```

#### Level nine cannot extend either word

There is only one west arrival on level six, namely its final `WL`.
Consequently level nine has exactly one west departure. Both level-eight
words have `5+e` north departures, and their fifth is `E4=EL`.

The sole west departure `W` on level nine has the four-step return

```text
W -> 6:WL -> 5:last SL -> 8:E4 -> fifth north arrival on 9.
```

If `W=SR`, there is no `NL` on level nine, and both endpoints turn right.
Thus `W=NL`, its target is `NR`, and consecutive stable order makes `W`
the fourth north arrival.

The fourth north departure on level eight is the `WR` immediately before
its final `WL,ER,EL` block. Call this event `Z`. There is therefore a
second four-step return

```text
Z -> 9:NL -> 6:WL -> 5:last SL -> 8:E4.
```

It has direction word `NWSE` and no intermediate level-eight visit. Its
endpoints would have to be consecutive, but both displayed level-eight
words have the intervening visits `WL,ER`. This contradiction excludes
the four-visit level-six branch, for either parity on levels eight and nine.

#### The remaining eight-visit level-six rows

Under the three non-wake assumptions, level six must therefore have

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(1,2,3,2).
```

The following short time comparison reduces its stable orders. Put
`A=tau(0:WR)`, `V=tau(2:WL)`, and `Z=tau(4:second WL)`. The forced return
`4:last WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL -> 4:EL` gives

```text
tau(4:last WR)=V-N-2,       Z<=V-N-6.
```

The third east arrival on level six, fed by `3:last SL`, has time
`Z+2-N<=V-2N-4`. The last north arrival on level five is at `V-N+3`,
and its last `SL` is later. Thus the last south departure on level six
occurs after its third east arrival and must be `WL`.

Use again the four-step return from the south predecessor of `5:SR` to
the second east arrival on level six. If that arrival is `EL`, its
opposite-turn predecessor is the unique `ER`, namely the first east arrival.
It is the first south departure for word A on level five and the second
for word B. If the second east arrival is `ER`, its predecessor is `WL`;
the resulting return to level five forces word A, since B has `NL` after
`SR`. Alternation, followed by the final-`WL` condition just proved, gives
the four remaining possibilities:

| Level-five word | Level-six word |
|---|---|
| A | `ER,EL,WR,EL,WR,WL,WR,WL` |
| A | `ER,EL,WR,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL` |
| B | `WR,WL,ER,EL,WR,EL,WR,WL` |
| A | `WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,EL,WR,WL` |

These four orders are the unresolved continuation of this even-band branch.
They are not a claim of existence and do not close the orbit.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-five-lower-bands-2026-09-13-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_five_lower_bands_2026-09-13

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_five_lower_bands_2026-09-13.md`.

### Five possible lower `(1,2,2)` bands through level six

For drift `(1,-3)`, every lower cap of profile `(1,2,2)` has one of
the five stable bands described below. No restriction on the period,
growth, upper cap, or later wake positions is used.

Put `b=omega_4`, `c=omega_5`, and `e=omega_6`. The common rows are

```text
0: WR
1: NR,SL
2: WR,WL
3: SR,SL,NR,SL
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,[WR]^b
5: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,(SL,NR)^b,[SL]^(1-c).
```

The level-six word is fixed by the wake indicators:

| `(b,c)` | Allowed `e` | Level six |
|---|---|---|
| `(0,1)` | `1` | `ER,EL,ER` |
| `(1,0)` | `0` or `1` | `ER,EL,ER,WL,WR,WL,[WR]^e` |
| `(1,1)` | `0` or `1` | `ER,EL,ER,WL,[WR]^e` |

The count of five includes the separate choices of `e` in the last
two rows. These bands are necessary conditions, not realizations.
The first row is the previously studied lower `0,5,6,8` branch.
The later note `odd_skew_fat122_adjacent_wakes_force_six_2026-09-13.md`
excludes `(b,c,e)=(1,1,0)`, and
`odd_skew_fat122_four_only_forces_six_2026-09-13.md` excludes
`(1,0,0)`. The later notes
`odd_skew_fat122_four_six_band_exclusion_2026-09-14.md` and
`odd_skew_fat122_adjacent_wake_band_exclusion_2026-09-14.md`
exclude `(1,0,1)` and `(1,1,1)` respectively. Only the first
band remains for this lower cap profile; the entire drift
orbit is still open.

#### The last east arrival cannot be a late south departure

Use `t=3x+y` and stable lifted times with

```text
tau(target)=tau(source)+1-N*lambda,
lambda(E)=1,       lambda(W)=-1,       lambda(N)=lambda(S)=0.
```

The count and last-east theorems in
`odd_skew_fat122_four_wake_single_er_2026-09-13.md` give

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(2,1,b-c+e,1+b-c)
```

on level six, and its last east arrival is `ER`. Denote that event
by `R`. The lower early-wake theorem rules out `b=c=0`.

For `b=0`, the same earlier results already force `c=e=1`, with
level-six word `ER,EL,ER` and the stated level-five word. Hence
assume `b=1` for the rest of the argument.

The two level-five possibilities before this reduction are

```text
A: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-c),
B: NR,SL,SR,NL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-c).
```

Let `W1,...,W5` be the west departures on level seven, feeding the
level-four west arrivals `WR,WL,WL,WR,WR`. The forced path

```text
W3 -> 4:second WL -> 3:last SL -> 6:R
```

has length three and zero longitudinal voltage. Thus

```text
tau(R)=tau(W3)+3.                                      (1)
```

Label the east arrivals on level eight by `Ei`. For either level-five
word, `E3,E4` are supplied by its second `NR` and second `SL`.
The seven-step path

```text
W4 -> 4:second WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL
   -> 4:EL -> 5:second NR -> E3
```

has zero longitudinal voltage. Since `W4` follows `W3`, it gives

```text
tau(E3)=tau(W4)+7 > tau(W3)+7.                          (2)
```

If `R` were the third south departure on level six, it would feed
`5:SL2` in either word. Consequently

```text
tau(E4)=tau(R)+2-N=tau(W3)+5-N.
```

This contradicts (2) and `E3<E4` in stable order. Therefore `R`
cannot be the third south departure.

A fourth south departure exists only when `c=0`. It feeds `5:SL3`,
which supplies `E6`. The preceding east arrival `E5` is supplied by
the last `NR` on level five. Its three-step path from `W5` gives

```text
tau(E5)=tau(W5)+3 > tau(W3)+3.
```

If `R` were that fourth south departure, (1) would instead give

```text
tau(E6)=tau(W3)+5-N < tau(W3)+3,
```

using `N>=6`. This contradicts `E5<E6`.

There is an earlier `ER` on level six, so `R` is not the first
south departure either. It must be the second.

#### The level-five order and the initial double square

In word B the second south arrival is `SR`. If the second south
departure `R` fed it, the four-step return

```text
6:R -> 5:SR -> 2:WR -> 3:NR -> second east arrival on 6
```

would end before its source, which is the third east arrival.
Thus word B is impossible: the level-five word is A.

The second south departure `R` now feeds `5:SL1`. The earlier `ER`
is necessarily the first south departure, feeding `5:SR`. Its
four-step return ends at the second east arrival, which must be
`EL`. The first `ER` and this `EL` are consecutive stable events.

No event can precede the first `ER`. It is both the first east
arrival and the first south departure, so any preceding event would
be `WR`; R-first alternation would then require a left turn before
this `ER`, but neither `EL` nor `WL` is available there. Nor can a
`WR` occur between the consecutive `ER,EL` pair and `R`: another
left turn would be needed, and a `WL` would create a south departure
before the second one `R`.

Hence the level-six word starts exactly

```text
ER,EL,ER.
```

All remaining ports are west arrivals. Alternation and the count row
now force the tails in the statement. This proves the five-band
classification.

The two gaps in this initial triple both equal four. The first is
the square through `5:SR,2:WR,3:NR`. For the second, the twelve-step
return from `7:W2` through the lower cap ends at its first north
arrival, fed by `6:EL`. Its source must be `SR`, and its consecutive
target is `NL=W3`. Therefore

```text
6:EL -> 7:W3=NL -> 4:second WL -> 3:last SL -> 6:last ER
```

is another four-step first same-cell return. If the first `ER` has
time `T`, the triple occurs at times `T,T+4,T+8`.

#### One further forced return when level four is wake

For `b=1`, the fourth west departure `W4` on level seven is `SR`.
To prove this, suppose it were `NL`. The first north arrival on
level seven is already `W3=NL`, supplied by `6:EL`. Thus `W4`
would have to be supplied by a `WR` event `X` on level six.

Every such `X` follows the first `WL`, denoted `J`, in the tails
just proved. The path from `W4` to `E3` then gives

```text
tau(E3)=tau(X)+8.
```

But `J`, the third south departure on level six, feeds `5:SL2`,
so `tau(E4)=tau(J)+2-N<tau(X)`. This contradicts `E3<E4`.
If no `WR` exists in the level-six tail, `W4=NL` is already
impossible by the north-arrival count. Hence `W4=SR` in every case.

Its seven-step path to `E3` forces `E3=ER`. Otherwise, the south
predecessor of `W4` would have an eight-step first return to
`E3=EL`, with source `E2=ER`. The path from `W1=SR` through
`4:first WR,5:first NR` to `E2` would then complete a four-step
same-cell return to `W4=SR`, with two right-turn endpoints.

The south step from `E3=ER` returns to the immediate stable
successor of `W4`, which is therefore `SL`. Thus `W4=SR` is
immediately followed by `SL`, independently of the later wakes.

All inequalities containing `-N` in this note are lower-cap
inequalities. Their signs reverse in upper inward coordinates.
The five-band classification and the last paragraph's forced
departure are not asserted for upper caps.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-four-only-forces-six-2026-09-13-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_four_only_forces_six_2026-09-13

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_four_only_forces_six_2026-09-13.md`.

### A lower wake at four cannot be followed by non-wakes at five and six

For a lower `(1,2,2)` cap in the `(1,-3)` orbit, the pattern

```text
(omega_4,omega_5,omega_6)=(1,0,0)
```

is impossible at every period. The proof uses exact returns through
level eleven and lifted-time order, with no hypotheses on later
wake indicators. Together with the preceding lower-cap results,
it shows that every lower `(1,2,2)` cap has a wake at level six.
The remaining three lower bands are not excluded here.

#### Fixed rows and notation

The lower-band and level-seven normal forms give

```text
0: WR
1: NR,SL
2: WR,WL
3: SR,SL,NR,SL
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,WR
5: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL,NR,SL
6: ER,EL,ER,WL,WR,WL.
```

Write `f=omega_7`. There are two possible level-seven words:

```text
P: SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,NR,SL,SR,[SL]^(1-f);
Q: SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,SR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-f).
```

Let `W1,...,W5` be their west departures. Their turns are
`SR,SR,NL,SR,SR`. Label the six east arrivals on level eight by
`E1,...,E6`, supplied by `5:SL1,NR1,NR2,SL2,NR3,SL3`.
The return from `W4` already forces `E3=ER`, the fifth south
departure on level eight.

There are three west departures `D1,D2,D3` on level nine, feeding
the west arrivals `WL,WR,WL` on level six. Label the east arrivals
on level ten as `Fi`. The three-step path from `D2` through
`6:WR,7:NR` ends at `F3` for P and at `F4` for Q.

Whenever a forced first same-cell return joins stable events, the
endpoints are consecutive and have opposite turns. Edge matching
preserves order. A path of length six and displacement `(1,-3)`
gives a stable-time difference `6-N`, which cannot join a class
event to a later stable event since `N>=6`.

#### Word P: the last west departure precedes the last south arrival

Suppose first that `E5=EL`. The four-step return through `W5`
forces the consecutive pair `E4=ER,E5=EL`. The path

```text
D1 -> 6:first WL -> 5:SL2 -> E4=ER -> 7:W5=SR
   -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR -> E5=EL -> north arrival on 9
```

is an eight-step first same-cell return. Its source cannot be
`NL`. If it were, its incoming predecessor `X` on level eight
would be the north departure immediately preceding `E5`, and

```text
8:X -> 9:D1=NL -> 6:first WL -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=ER
```

would force `X=EL` to be the preceding east arrival `E3`, contrary
to `E3=ER`. Hence `D1=SR`, followed immediately by `NL=D2`.

If `F3=EL`, the south predecessor of `D1` on level ten has a
twelve-step first return, following the eight-step return to
`D2` and its three-step path to `F3`. Its source is the preceding
east arrival `F2=ER`, supplied by the `SL` after `7:W4`.
This forces

```text
8:E3=ER -> 7:SL2 -> 10:F2=ER -> 9:D1=SR
        -> 6:first WL -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=ER.
```

The direction word is `SESWSE`, so it contradicts `E3<E4` in
stable order. Thus `F3=ER`.

Its south step completes a four-step return from `D2=NL` to a
new `SR`, necessarily `D3`. If `E6=EL`, the four-step return
from `D3` through `6:last WL,5:last SL,E6` would end at another
`NL`, a fourth west departure on level nine. Therefore `E6=ER`.

Since `E4=ER` feeds `W5`, the later south departure `E6=ER` must
feed a following `SL`. This forces `f=0`. That last `SL` on
level seven supplies `F5`, and the now forced path

```text
10:F3=ER -> 9:D3=SR -> 6:last WL -> 5:last SL
         -> 8:E6=ER -> 7:last SL -> 10:F5
```

has length six and displacement `(1,-3)`. It contradicts `F3<F5`.
Thus `E5=EL` is impossible in P.

It remains to take `E5=ER`. Its south step returns from the last
right turn `W5=SR` to a following `SL`, forcing `f=0`. Thus `E5`
is the last south departure on level eight. The later east arrival
is necessarily `E6=EL`, immediately following `E5`: any intervening
left turn would be another east arrival or another south departure.

The four-step return from `D3` through `6:last WL,5:last SL,E6`
forces `D3=NL`, since an `SR` source would create a further west
departure at its `NL` target. Its incoming predecessor on level
eight must then be `WR`, immediately preceding `E6=EL` in stable
order. That event is instead `E5=ER`. This excludes P.

#### Word Q: the remaining north arrival comes last

In Q, `W5` is the sixth south arrival on level seven, whereas
`E3=ER` is the fifth south departure on level eight. If `E5=EL`,
its return through `W5` would force `E4=ER` as the sixth south
departure. But alternation between the consecutive east arrivals
`E3=ER,E4=ER` requires an intervening `WL`, inserting another
south departure between ordinals five and six. Hence `E5=ER`.

Its south step feeds the `SL` immediately after `W5`, so `E5` is
the seventh south departure. The sixth departure is a `WL`, denoted
`J`, immediately followed by `E5` in stable order. As in the
adjacent-wake exclusion, these south ordinals force `E4=EL` and
the consecutive pair `E3=ER,E4=EL`.

The four-step return from `D1` through
`6:first WL,5:SL2,E4=EL` rules out `D1=NL`: its incoming north
predecessor on level eight would have to be the immediately
preceding `WR`, but the preceding stable event is `E3=ER`.
Thus `D1=SR`, with consecutive target `NL=D2`.

If `F4=ER`, its south step would return from `D2=NL` to
`D3=SR`. The last-west-departure argument would then force
`E6=ER`. But `E5` is already the seventh south departure, and
Q has at most eight south arrivals. Alternation between the
consecutive east arrivals `E5=ER,E6=ER` would insert a `WL`,
putting `E6` at ordinal at least nine. Therefore `F4=EL`.

The eight-step first return from the south predecessor of `D1`
through `D2` to `F4` consequently forces the consecutive pair

```text
F3=ER,F4=EL.
```

Here `F3` is supplied by the `SL` immediately after `W5`, hence
by the south departure `E5=ER` on level eight.

The same south-ordinal argument shows that `E6=ER` is impossible,
whether or not `F4` turns right. Hence `E6=EL`, and the four-step
return from `D3` through `6:last WL,5:last SL,E6` forces `D3=NL`.
Its incoming predecessor is `WR`, immediately preceding `E6`.

There must therefore be one more south departure after `E5` and
before `E6`; otherwise `E5=ER,E6=EL` would be consecutive. It
is the `WL` supplying the final `SL` after `7:NR`, so `f=0`.
Call it `K`. The tail on level eight is now fixed as

```text
E3=ER,E4=EL,X1=WR,J=WL,E5=ER,K=WL,X2=WR,E6=EL,[WR]^g,
g=omega_8.
```

#### The last east arrival on level ten

The north departure `E4=EL` feeds `D2=NL`; the next north
departure `X1=WR` feeds an `NR`. The following one, `X2=WR`,
feeds `D3=NL`, whose consecutive target is the `NR` supplied by
`E6=EL`. There is no south arrival between these events: a left
south arrival would require another right turn before the next
specified north arrival, while all the remaining west departures
are already the two specified `NL` ports. Thus every south arrival
on level nine after `D1=SR` occurs after the north arrival from
`E6`.

The last east arrival `F5` on level ten is supplied by
`8:K -> 7:last SL`. Its time is

```text
tau(F5)=tau(K)+2-N < tau(E6).
```

If it turned right, it would feed a south arrival after `D1`,
which, by the preceding paragraph, is later than
`tau(E6)+1`. This is impossible. Hence `F5=EL`.

The same comparison puts every south departure on level ten
after `F3=ER` later than `F5`. Therefore there is no `WL`
between `F4=EL` and `F5=EL`; alternation gives the consecutive
block

```text
F4=EL,WR,F5=EL.                                       (1)
```

#### Two adjacent west departures on level eleven

The level-eight west arrivals `J=WL,K=WL` are adjacent in west
arrival order. Their matching west departures on level eleven,
denoted `P1,P2`, are consequently adjacent. The first has the
sixteen-step first same-cell return

```text
P1 -> 8:J=WL -> 7:W5=SR -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR
   -> 8:E5=ER -> 7:SL3 -> 10:F3=ER -> 9:D1=SR
   -> 6:first WL -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=EL -> 9:D2=NL
   -> 6:WR -> 7:NR -> 10:F4=EL -> north arrival on 11.
```

The second has the four-step first return

```text
P2 -> 8:K=WL -> 7:last SL -> 10:F5=EL
   -> north arrival on 11.
```

Write their north targets as `Na,Nb`. Block (1) gives `b=a+2`.
If `P1=SR`, its consecutive target `Na=NL` would be `P2`,
whose return would then have to end at `N(a+1)`. Therefore
`P1=NL=N(a-1)`.

Let `X` be its incoming predecessor on level ten. It is the north
departure immediately preceding `F4`. Since `F3=ER,F4=EL` are
consecutive stable events, `X` precedes `F3`. The first part of
the long return gives the eight-step first same-cell return

```text
10:X -> 11:P1=NL -> 8:J=WL -> 7:W5=SR -> 4:last WR
     -> 5:last NR -> 8:E5=ER -> 7:SL3 -> 10:F3=ER.
```

Its source must be `EL`, immediately preceding `F3`. Thus
`X=F2=EL`, supplied by the `SL` after `7:W4`, whose predecessor
is `8:E3=ER`. It follows that

```text
8:E3=ER -> 7:SL2 -> 10:F2=EL -> 11:P1=NL -> 8:J=WL
```

is a four-step first same-cell return. But `E4=EL,X1=WR` lie
strictly between `E3` and `J` in the forced level-eight tail.
This final skipped-visit contradiction excludes Q.

#### Remaining lower bands

Both level-seven continuations are impossible, so `(1,0,0)` is
excluded. The pattern `(1,1,0)` was excluded in
`odd_skew_fat122_adjacent_wakes_force_six_2026-09-13.md`, and
`omega_4=0` already forces `(omega_5,omega_6)=(1,1)`.
Every lower `(1,2,2)` cap therefore has `omega_6=1`.

The remaining wake-indicator triples are

```text
(omega_4,omega_5,omega_6)=(0,1,1), (1,0,1), or (1,1,1).
```

This does not exclude these three bands or the other cap profiles
in the `(1,-3)` orbit. The lifted-time comparisons in the proof
are lower-cap comparisons and have not been transferred to the
upper orientation.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-four-six-band-exclusion-2026-09-14-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_four_six_band_exclusion_2026-09-14

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_four_six_band_exclusion_2026-09-14.md`.

### Excluding the lower four-six wake band

At a lower `(1,2,2)` cap in the `(1,-3)` orbit, the band

```text
(omega_4,omega_5,omega_6)=(1,0,1)
```

is impossible. The forced tail from
`odd_skew_fat122_four_six_wake_order_2026-09-14.md` has a
twelve-step first return whose endpoints both turn right.
The contradiction is independent of the period and of all
later wake indicators.

Together with the earlier exclusions, this proves that every
lower `(1,2,2)` cap has wakes at both levels five and six.
The remaining early triples are `(0,1,1)` and `(1,1,1)`.
Neither those bands nor the other cap profiles in the orbit
are excluded by this note.

#### The forced part of the tail

Use the notation and reductions of the preceding note. The
remaining level-seven word is

```text
SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,SR,SL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-f).
```

The relevant tail on level eight is

```text
E3=ER,E4=EL,X1=WR,J=WL,E5=ER,E6=EL,X2=WR,K=WL,... .
```

The four west departures on level nine are
`D1=SR,D2=NL,D3=SR,D4=NL`, feeding the four west
arrivals `WL1,WR1,WL2,WR2` on level six. On level ten,
`F3=ER,F4=EL` are consecutive; they are supplied by
`7:SL3,NR2`. The preceding east arrival `F2` is supplied
by `7:SL2`, whose incoming predecessor is `8:E3`.

Let `P_J,P_X` be the west departures on level eleven
feeding `8:J,8:X2`. They are adjacent in west-departure
order, since no west arrival lies between `J` and `X2`.

#### The north departure from F4 feeds P_X

The forced path from `P_J` is the sixteen-step first return

```text
11:P_J -> 8:J=WL -> 7:W5=SR -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR
       -> 8:E5=ER -> 7:SL3 -> 10:F3=ER -> 9:D1=SR
       -> 6:WL1 -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=EL -> 9:D2=NL
       -> 6:WR1 -> 7:NR2 -> 10:F4=EL -> north arrival on 11.
```

Suppose `P_J=NL`. Its incoming predecessor `X` on level
ten is then the north departure immediately preceding
`F4`. Because `F3=ER,F4=EL` are consecutive, `X` precedes
`F3`. The beginning of the same path gives the eight-step
first return

```text
10:X -> 11:P_J=NL -> 8:J=WL -> 7:W5=SR -> 4:last WR
     -> 5:last NR -> 8:E5=ER -> 7:SL3 -> 10:F3=ER.
```

Its endpoints must be consecutive and have opposite turns,
so `X=F2=EL`. This forces the square return

```text
8:E3=ER -> 7:SL2 -> 10:F2=EL -> 11:P_J=NL -> 8:J=WL.
```

It skips the intervening stable events `E4,X1`, a
contradiction. Therefore `P_J=SR`. The endpoint of its
sixteen-step return is consequently the next stable event
`NL`. It is also the next west departure, namely `P_X`.
Thus the north departure from `10:F4=EL` feeds
`11:P_X=NL`, which feeds `8:X2=WR`.

#### The twelve-step contradiction

Starting at `8:E5=ER`, the now prescribed path is

```text
8:E5=ER -> 7:SL3 -> 10:F3=ER -> 9:D1=SR -> 6:WL1
        -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=EL -> 9:D2=NL -> 6:WR1
        -> 7:NR2 -> 10:F4=EL -> 11:P_X=NL -> 8:X2=WR.
```

Its direction word is

```text
S E S W S E N W N E N W.
```

Taking the starting cell as `(0,0)`, the successive cells are

```text
(0,0), (0,-1), (1,-1), (1,-2), (0,-2), (0,-3), (1,-3),
(1,-2), (0,-2), (0,-1), (1,-1), (1,0), (0,0).
```

Thus the endpoint is the first return to the starting
physical cell, not merely a translated copy of it. Both
endpoint ports, `E5=ER` and `X2=WR`, turn right, whereas
successive visits to a cell must alternate their turns.
Equivalently, the forced return skips the stable event
`E6=EL` between them. This excludes the tail and hence
the entire `(1,0,1)` lower-cap band.

This last contradiction has zero displacement. Its use here
still depends on the preceding lower-orientation reductions;
the complete band exclusion has not been asserted for an
upper cap.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-four-six-wake-order-2026-09-14-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_four_six_wake_order_2026-09-14

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_four_six_wake_order_2026-09-14.md`.

### A forced tail when four and six are wake and five is not

Consider a lower `(1,2,2)` cap in the `(1,-3)` orbit with

```text
(omega_4,omega_5,omega_6)=(1,0,1).
```

Both remaining north right turns on level seven must follow its
last west departure. The alternative with one on either side is
excluded below. In the surviving order, write `f=omega_7` and
`g=omega_8`. Level seven is

```text
SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,SR,SL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-f).
```

If `E1,...,E6` denote the east arrivals on level eight, its tail
from `E3` is necessarily

```text
E3=ER,E4=EL,X1=WR,J=WL,E5=ER,E6=EL,X2=WR,K=WL,
    (WR,WL)^(1-f),[WR]^g.                              (1)
```

Moreover, `f=1` forces `g=1`. These are all-period lower-cap
restrictions, not an exclusion of the surviving tail. The proof
uses prescribed first returns and lifted-time order, without a
finite search or assumptions on later wakes.

#### Fixed rows and clocks

The preceding lower-band and level-seven notes give

```text
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,WR
5: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL,NR,SL
6: ER,EL,ER,WL1,WR1,WL2,WR2.
```

The west departures `W1,...,W5` on level seven have turns
`SR,SR,NL,SR,SR`. The two remaining candidate words are

```text
P: SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,NR,SL,SR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-f);
Q: SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,SR,SL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-f).
```

There are six east arrivals `Ei` on level eight, supplied in
order by the east departures `SL1,NR1,NR2,SL2,NR3,SL3` on
level five. In particular, `E3=ER` is already forced and is
the fifth south departure on level eight. Level eight has
`9-f` south departures in total.

Let `D1,...,D4` be the west departures on level nine, feeding
`6:WL1,WR1,WL2,WR2`. Write `Fi` for the east arrivals on
level ten. There are `7-f` of them. The three-step paths from
`D2,D4` through a level-six `WR` and a level-seven `NR` end at

```text
             from D2     from D4
P:              F3          F6
Q:              F4          F6.
```

The following comparisons will also be used:

```text
tau(E4) = tau(6:WL1)+2-N
tau(E6) = tau(6:WL2)+2-N
tau(F6) = tau(6:WR2)+2-N > tau(E6).                   (2)
```

For P, `tau(F3)=tau(6:WR1)+2-N > tau(E4)`; for Q the
same inequality holds with `F4` in place of `F3`.

A south departure `I` on level eight which feeds an `SL` on
level seven supplies the corresponding `F` at time
`tau(F)=tau(I)+2-N`. Throughout, `N>=6`. Thus a six-step
path with displacement `(1,-3)` cannot run from one stable
event to a later event of the same class.

Every same-cell return used below is a first return: its listed
path has no earlier visit to its starting cell. Its endpoints
must therefore be consecutive stable events with opposite turns.

#### An obstruction at the last two west departures on nine

Suppose `D3=SR` and `E6=EL`. The square through
`6:WL2,5:SL3,E6` forces the consecutive target `D4=NL`.
Then `F6` cannot be `ER`: the square

```text
D4=NL -> 6:WR2 -> 7:last NR -> F6=ER -> south arrival on 9
```

would end at a fifth west departure `SR`. Hence `F6=EL`.

The south predecessor `Y` of `D3` on level ten has the
eight-step first return

```text
10:Y -> 9:D3=SR -> 6:WL2 -> 5:SL3 -> 8:E6=EL
     -> 9:D4=NL -> 6:WR2 -> 7:last NR -> 10:F6=EL.
```

It follows that `Y=F5=ER`, immediately preceding `F6`.
In particular, a square which identifies `Y` with `F3` or
`F4` gives a skipped-visit contradiction.

#### Excluding P when E5 turns left

If `E5=EL`, the square through `W5` forces the consecutive
pair `E4=ER,E5=EL`. There is an eight-step return

```text
D1 -> 6:WL1 -> 5:SL2 -> E4=ER -> 7:W5=SR
   -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR -> E5=EL -> north arrival on 9.
```

Its source cannot be `NL`. In that case its incoming north
predecessor `X` on level eight would be the north departure
immediately preceding `E5`, and the square
`X -> D1 -> 6:WL1 -> 5:SL2 -> E4=ER` would force
`X=EL=E3`, contrary to `E3=ER`. Thus `D1=SR`, followed
immediately by `D2=NL`.

If `F3=EL`, the south predecessor of `D1` on level ten follows
this eight-step return and then the path from `D2` to `F3`.
The resulting twelve-step first return forces that predecessor
to be `F2=ER`. But `F2` is supplied by the `SL` after `W4`,
so this forces the six-step translated path

```text
8:E3=ER -> 7:SL2 -> 10:F2=ER -> 9:D1=SR
        -> 6:WL1 -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4=ER.
```

This contradicts `E3<E4`. Therefore `F3=ER`, whose square
from `D2=NL` forces `D3=SR`.

If `E6=EL`, the preceding obstruction would identify the
south predecessor of `D3` both as `F3` and as `F5`. Hence
`E6=ER`. The south ordinal of `E4` is seven, so `E6` feeds
one of the two possible later `SL` ports on level seven.
They supply `F5` and, when present, `F7`. Thus

```text
10:F3=ER -> 9:D3=SR -> 6:WL2 -> 5:SL3 -> 8:E6=ER
         -> 7:SL -> 10:F5 or F7
```

is another six-step translated path to a later east arrival.
This excludes `E5=EL` in P.

#### Excluding P when E5 turns right

If `E5=ER`, its square from `W5=SR` ends at the following
`SL`. Consequently `E5` is the eighth south departure.
Since `E3=ER` is the fifth, `E4` must be `EL`: taking `E4=ER`
would require a `WL` between each successive pair of east
right turns and put `E5` at south ordinal at least nine.
Likewise `E6=ER` would require a further intervening `WL`
and put `E6` at ordinal at least ten. Hence `E6=EL`.

If `E3,E4` were not consecutive stable events, a `WL` between
them would be the sixth south departure `I`. It feeds the
`SL` before `W5`, hence supplies `F4`. By (2),

```text
tau(F4)=tau(I)+2-N < tau(E4) < tau(F3),
```

contradicting `F3<F4`. Therefore `E3=ER,E4=EL` are
consecutive.

The square from `D1` through `6:WL1,5:SL2,E4=EL` now
forces `D1=SR,D2=NL`. Indeed, an `NL` source would require
an incoming `WR` immediately before `E4`, where the stable
predecessor is `E3=ER`.

Taking `F3=ER` would force `D3=SR`, contradicting the
obstruction with `E6=EL`. Taking `F3=EL` instead gives an
eight-step first return from the south predecessor of `D1`
to `F3`. This forces `F2=ER` and the same forbidden
six-step translated path from `E3` to `E4`. P is excluded.

#### The last east arrival in Q

In Q, `W5` is the sixth south arrival on level seven. Taking
`E5=EL` would force `E4=ER` as the sixth south departure
on level eight. This is impossible after `E3=ER` at ordinal
five: an intervening `WL` is required. Thus `E5=ER`, at
south ordinal seven.

Alternation now forces

```text
E3=ER,E4=EL,X1=WR,J=WL,E5=ER,                         (3)
```

where `J` is the sixth south departure and feeds `W5`.
In particular, `D1=SR,D2=NL` as in the preceding section.

If `E6=ER`, it must be the ninth south departure, forcing
`f=0`. It then supplies the last east arrival `F7` through
the last `SL` on level seven. But (2) would give

```text
tau(F7)=tau(E6)+2-N < tau(E6) < tau(F6),
```

contrary to `F6<F7`. Hence `E6=EL`. More generally, when
`f=0`, the ninth south departure must occur after `E6`, by
the same comparison.

Taking `F4=ER` would force `D3=SR`, with south predecessor
`F4`, contrary to the last-two-departures obstruction. Thus
`F4=EL`. The eight-step return from the south predecessor
of `D1` forces the consecutive pair

```text
F3=ER,F4=EL.                                        (4)
```

Here `F3` is supplied by `E5` through the `SL` after `W5`.

#### The eighth south departure cannot precede E6

Let `K` be the eighth south departure on level eight. It is
`WL` and supplies `F5`. Suppose `K<E6`. Since the ninth
south departure, if present, is after `E6`, the tail through
`E6` must be

```text
E3=ER,E4=EL,X1=WR,J=WL,E5=ER,K=WL,X2=WR,E6=EL.
```

If `D3=SR`, its south predecessor is `F5=ER` by the
obstruction. This gives a six-step translated path from
`K` through `7:SL,10:F5,9:D3,6:WL2,5:SL3` to `E6`,
contradicting `K<E6`. Hence `D3=NL`.

The north arrival from `E4` is `D2=NL`; the one from `X1`
is `NR`; the one from `X2` is `D3=NL`, immediately
followed by `NR` from `E6`. There is no south arrival
between these events. Any such arrival would require an
additional `SR` before `D3`, whereas the intervening west
departures are already `D2,D3`. The fourth west departure
occurs later. Thus every south arrival after `D1` is later
than the north arrival from `E6`.

Since `tau(F5)=tau(K)+2-N<tau(E6)`, it follows that
`F5=EL`. The same comparison excludes all south departures
between `F4` and `F5` on level ten, forcing the consecutive
block

```text
F4=EL,WR,F5=EL.                                     (5)
```

The west arrivals `J,K` are adjacent in west-arrival order
on level eight. Let their matching west departures on
level eleven be `P1,P2`. They have first returns

```text
P1 -> 8:J -> 7:W5 -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR -> 8:E5
   -> 7:SL3 -> 10:F3 -> 9:D1 -> 6:WL1 -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4
   -> 9:D2 -> 6:WR1 -> 7:NR2 -> 10:F4 -> north arrival Na;

P2 -> 8:K -> 7:SL4 -> 10:F5 -> north arrival Nb.
```

These have lengths sixteen and four. Block (5) gives
`b=a+2`. If `P1=SR`, its consecutive target `Na=NL`
would be `P2`, whose return would then have to end at
`N(a+1)`. Therefore `P1=NL=N(a-1)`.

Its incoming north predecessor `X` on level ten precedes
`F3`, by (4). The initial part of its long return gives

```text
10:X -> 11:P1 -> 8:J -> 7:W5 -> 4:last WR
     -> 5:last NR -> 8:E5 -> 7:SL3 -> 10:F3=ER.
```

This eight-step first return forces `X=F2=EL`. But then

```text
8:E3=ER -> 7:SL2 -> 10:F2=EL -> 11:P1=NL -> 8:J=WL
```

is a square return skipping `E4,X1` in the stable word.
This contradiction proves `E6<K` and establishes (1).

#### A wake at seven forces a wake at eight

In the surviving tail, `E5=ER,E6=EL` are consecutive.
The square from `D3` through `6:WL2,5:SL3,E6` therefore
cannot have an `NL` source: its incoming predecessor on
level eight would have to be `WR` immediately before
`E6`. Thus `D3=SR,D4=NL`, and the obstruction forces
the consecutive pair `F5=ER,F6=EL`.

On level eleven, let `P_J,P_X,P_K` feed the successive
west arrivals `J,X2,K` on level eight. The sixteen-step
return from `P_J` to the north arrival supplied by `F4`
is unchanged. Taking `P_J=NL` again forces the square
from `E3` to `J` which skips `E4,X1`. Hence `P_J=SR`,
and its consecutive target is `P_X=NL`, supplied by
`10:F4=EL`.

There is also a twelve-step first return

```text
P_K -> 8:K -> 7:SL4 -> 10:F5=ER -> 9:D3=SR -> 6:WL2
    -> 5:SL3 -> 8:E6=EL -> 9:D4=NL -> 6:WR2
    -> 7:NR3 -> 10:F6=EL -> north arrival on 11.
```

If `P_K=NL`, its incoming predecessor `X` on level ten
is the north departure immediately preceding `F6`.
The square `X -> P_K -> 8:K -> 7:SL4 -> F5=ER`
would force `X=F4=EL`. But this departure already feeds
the distinct west departure `P_X`. Thus `P_K=SR`.
Its consecutive target is a further west departure `NL`.
There must consequently be a west arrival after `K` on
level eight. In (1), when `f=1`, the only possible such
arrival is the final `[WR]^g`. Hence `g=1`.

The surviving tail (1), with `f=0` or `(f,g)=(1,1)`, is
subsequently excluded in
`odd_skew_fat122_four_six_band_exclusion_2026-09-14.md`.
These lower-orientation clock comparisons have not been
transferred to an upper cap.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-four-wake-single-er-2026-09-13-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_four_wake_single_er_2026-09-13

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_four_wake_single_er_2026-09-13.md`.

### The single-`ER` branch after a wake at offset four

Consider a `(1,2,2)` cap in the `(1,-3)` orbit, with inward wake
indicator `omega_4=1`. Put `c=omega_5` and `e=omega_6`. If offset six
has exactly one `ER` port, the following are necessary stable words:

```text
5: NR,SL,SR,NL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-c);

c=0:
6 T: WR,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,EL,WR,WL,[WR]^e;
6 U: WR,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,[WR]^e.

c=1:
6 V: WR,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,EL,[WR]^e.
```

This restriction holds at either cap, with heading labels rotated at
the upper cap. It does not require growth eight, a bound on the period,
or any wake information beyond offset six.

At a lower cap, all these words are impossible. This last conclusion
uses the sign of the longitudinal voltage and is not asserted for the
upper cap. Combined with the earlier wake-five argument, it proves
that every lower `(1,2,2)` cap has exactly two `ER` ports at offset six.

The upper words T and V are subsequently excluded by a zero-
displacement return in
`odd_skew_upper_a_single_er_reduction_2026-09-14.md`.
For family A, that note also reduces U to a wake at upper offset
six and one fixed offset-seven word. The two-`ER` continuation,
this remaining U order, and family A itself remain open.

#### The square and the possible prefixes

The two-sided `(1,2,2)` normal form gives

```text
0: WR
1: NR,SL
2: WR,WL
3: SR,SL,NR,SL
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,WR
5 A: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-c)
  B: NR,SL,SR,NL,NR,SL,NR,[SL]^(1-c).
```

With one `ER` on offset six, its port counts are

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(1,2,4-c+e,3-c).
```

The south predecessor of `5:SR` has the four-step first same-cell
return through `5:SR,2:WR,3:NR` to the second east arrival on offset
six. If that arrival is `EL`, its consecutive predecessor is the first
east arrival `ER`. If it is `ER`, the predecessor is `WL`, with the
first east arrival `EL` earlier in the word.

For word A, this predecessor is the first south departure. For word B,
it is the second. Alternation gives the following prefix groups; all
unspecified right turns are `WR`:

| Word at five | Second east arrival | Prefix at six | Remaining left turns |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | `EL` | `ER,EL` | one `EL`, `3-c` copies of `WL` |
| B | `EL` | `WR,WL,ER,EL` | one `EL`, `2-c` copies of `WL` |
| A | `ER` | `WR,EL,WR,WL,ER` | one `EL`, `2-c` copies of `WL` |
| B | `ER` | `WR,EL,WR,WL,WR,WL,ER` | one `EL`, `1-c` copies of `WL` |
| B | `ER` | `WR,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL,ER` | one `EL`, `1-c` copies of `WL` |

The table follows directly by placing the first `EL` and the south
predecessor before the second east arrival. In the last two groups,
one `WL` must occur before that predecessor; the first `EL` can lie
on either side of this earlier `WL`. Every row ends with a left turn
when `e=0`, or an additional final `WR` when `e=1`.

#### The first north-return pair

Let `W1,...,W5` be the west departures on offset seven. Their targets
on offset four have ordered headings

```text
WR,WL,WL,WR,WR.
```

Write `Ni` for the north arrivals on offset seven. There are two fixed
returns. The first starts

```text
W2 -> 4:first WL -> 3:SR -> 0:WR -> 1:NR -> 4:ER
   -> 3:first SL -> first east arrival on 6.
```

If this east arrival is `EL`, its north step completes an eight-step
return. Otherwise the path continues through `5:SR,2:WR,3:NR` to the
second east arrival `EL`, then north, completing a twelve-step return.
The second fixed return is

```text
W3 -> 4:second WL -> 3:last SL -> last EL on 6 -> north arrival on 7.
```

It has length four. Neither return has an intermediate offset-seven
visit, and both have zero displacement.

Suppose these adjacent west departures return to `Na,Nb`. If `W2=SR`,
its consecutive target `Na=NL` is `W3`; the second return must then
end at `N(a+1)`. Thus, whenever `b!=a+1`, one instead has
`W2=NL=N(a-1)`. In particular, `a=1` and `b!=2` are incompatible.

For the first prefix group in the table, the return targets are `N1`
and one of `N3,...,N6`. This excludes that group. For the second group
they are `N2` and one of `N4,...,N6`. Hence `W2=NL=N1`, producing
the eight-step return

```text
6:first WR -> 7:W2 -> 4:first WL -> 3:SR -> 0:WR
           -> 1:NR -> 4:ER -> 3:first SL -> 6:first ER.
```

Both endpoints turn right. The second group is therefore impossible
as well.

#### An immediate `SR,NL` pair at offset seven

For the third and fourth prefix groups, the return from `W2` ends at
`N2`; the one from `W3` ends at an ordinal at least four. Thus
`W2=NL=N1`. The earlier west departure `W1` is `SR`, immediately
followed by this `NL`. An intervening stable event would require
either another west departure or a north arrival before `N1`.

Consider the fourth prefix group, whose offset-five word is B. The
three-step path from `W1` through `4:first WR,5:first NR` reaches the
first east arrival on offset eight. Its south-departure predecessor
has a four-step return to that first east arrival. The predecessor
cannot be `ER`, since it lies before the first east arrival, so it is
`WL`; the first east arrival is consequently `ER`.

Its south step now completes a four-step return from `W1=SR` to a
south arrival, contradicting the immediate `NL` successor of `W1`.
This excludes the fourth prefix group.

For the third group, the offset-five word is A. Label the east
arrivals on offset eight by `Ei`, fed in order by

```text
5:SL1,NR1,NR2,SL2,NR3,[SL3]^(1-c).
```

The same three-step path from `W1` now ends at `E2`. Its south step
cannot return to the immediate successor `NL`, so `E2=EL`. The source
of the four-step return to it must be `E1=ER`. They are consecutive
stable events, and they are the initial two events on offset eight:
their source is the first south departure, while no west arrival can
occur earlier without an intervening left turn and an earlier south
departure.

The third group consists of the following words, each followed by
`[WR]^e`. The final word C3 occurs only for `c=0`:

```text
C1: WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,EL,(WR,WL)^(2-c);
C2: WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,(WR,WL)^(1-c);
C3: WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,WL,WR,EL.
```

#### The word C1 is impossible

For C1, the second fixed return is `W3 -> N4`. If `W3=NL`, its source
is `N3`, fed by the second `WR` on offset six. The resulting four-step
return to the last `EL` there skips `WL,ER`. Hence `W3=SR`, followed
immediately by `N4=NL=W4`.

The path

```text
W4 -> 4:second WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL
   -> 4:EL -> 5:second NR -> E3
```

has length seven. If `E3=ER`, its south step returns to the next
stable event after `W4=NL`. That event must be `SR`, necessarily `W5`.
But then

```text
8:E3=ER -> 7:W5=SR -> 4:last WR -> 5:last NR -> 8:E5
```

is a four-step first return that skips `E4`. Thus `E3=EL`.

The south predecessor of `W3` on offset eight follows the four-step
return to `W4`, then this seven-step path to `E3`. It has a twelve-step
first return, forcing its source to be the immediately preceding
east arrival `E2=ER`. This contradicts the already established
`E2=EL` and excludes C1.

#### The adjacent `WL` ports exclude C2 and C3

In either C2 or C3, the first four west arrivals on offset six are

```text
WR,WR,WL,WL.
```

Let `D1,D2,...` be the west departures on offset nine feeding them.
The third has the twelve-step first return

```text
D3 -> 6:first WL -> 5:SR -> 2:WR -> 3:NR -> 6:ER
   -> 5:first SL -> 8:E1=ER -> 7:W1=SR -> 4:first WR
   -> 5:first NR -> 8:E2=EL -> first north arrival on 9.
```

The target is the first north arrival because `E1=ER,E2=EL` are the
initial events on offset eight. The source cannot be `NL` before
that first north arrival. Hence `D3=SR`, and its consecutive target
is `NL=D4`.

It follows that

```text
8:E2=EL -> 9:D4=NL -> 6:second WL -> 5:second SL -> 8:E4
```

is a four-step first same-cell return. It skips `E3` in east-arrival
order, which is impossible. This excludes C2 and C3, completing the
exclusion of the third prefix group.

#### The last prefix group

Only the fifth group remains. For `c=0`, its remaining `EL` and `WL`
can occur in either order, giving words T and U. For `c=1`, only the
final `EL` remains, giving word V. The first north-return pair gives

```text
W2=NL=N2;
T: W3 returns to N5;
U: W3 returns to N6.
V: W3 returns to N5.
```

In T and V, `W3=NL` would mean `W3=N4`, fed by the third `WR` on
offset six. The return to its last `EL` would skip `WL,ER`. Thus
these words require `W3=SR`, with consecutive target `NL=W4`.
For U, either turn at `W3` has not yet been excluded.

#### The delayed fourth west departure excludes the lower words

Let `L` be the last `EL` on offset six and `R` its unique `ER`.
In every remaining word, `R` precedes `L`. The fixed four-step
return from `W3` ends at the north arrival fed by `L`.

If `W3=SR`, this target is `NL=W4`. If `W3=NL`, its target is the
immediately following `NR`, and `W4` occurs later. In either case,
writing stable lifted times in the lower-cap coordinates gives

```text
tau(W4) >= tau(L)+1.
```

The offset-five word is B. Its third and fourth east departures are
`NR2` and `SL2`; call the corresponding east arrivals on offset eight
`E3,E4`. The fixed path

```text
W4 -> 4:second WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL
   -> 4:EL -> 5:second NR -> E3
```

has length seven and zero longitudinal voltage. Hence

```text
tau(E3) = tau(W4)+7 >= tau(L)+8.
```

The first three south departures on offset six are `WL,WL,ER`.
They feed `5:SL1,SR,SL2`, respectively. Thus `R` supplies `E4`
through the two-step path `6:R -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4`. At the lower cap
this path has voltage one, giving

```text
tau(E4) = tau(R)+2-N.
```

Since `tau(R)<tau(L)` and `N>=6`, these two equations imply
`tau(E4)<tau(E3)`. But `E3` is the third east arrival and `E4` the
fourth. Order-preserving edge matching requires the opposite strict
inequality. This excludes every lower word T, U, and V.

At the upper cap the last equation is instead
`tau(E4)=tau(R)+2+N`. There is no contradiction from these bounds
in that orientation. The upper continuations remain open.

#### Consequence for every lower `(1,2,2)` cap

Put `b=omega_4`, retaining `c=omega_5` and `e=omega_6`. If `z` is
the number of `ER` ports on offset six, the general count row is

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(z,3-z,6+b-c+e-3z,3+b-c-z).
```

Nonnegative counts give `z<=2`. The square through
`5:SR,2:WR,3:NR` excludes `z=0`, since both endpoints would turn
left. For `b=1`, the preceding proof excludes `z=1` for either
value of `c`.

For `b=0`, the lower early-wake theorem forces `c=1`, and
`odd_skew_fat122_rigid_five_wake_2026-09-13.md` already excludes
`z=1`. Consequently every lower `(1,2,2)` cap has `z=2`, with

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(2,1,b-c+e,1+b-c).
```

When `(b,c)=(0,1)`, nonnegativity forces `e=1` and three visits.
When `(b,c)=(1,0)`, offset six has `6+e` visits. When `(b,c)=(1,1)`,
it has `4+e` visits. This is a uniform local restriction on the
lower fat-cap profile, not an exclusion of that profile or of the
whole `(1,-3)` orbit.

#### The last east arrival also turns right

Every lower `(1,2,2)` cap has `ER` as the last east arrival on offset
six. For `b=0`, this follows from its already forced word `ER,EL,ER`.
Suppose `b=1` and, to the contrary, the last east arrival is `EL`.
The first two east arrivals are then both `ER`.

The square through `5:SR,2:WR,3:NR` ends at the second of these
east arrivals. Its consecutive predecessor is `WL`. The first `ER`
is an earlier south departure, so `5:SR` cannot be the first south
arrival. Therefore the offset-five word is B: the first south
arrival is `SL1`, the second is `SR`, and the third is `SL2`.
The south departures supplying them are respectively the first
`ER`, its later `WL`, and the second `ER` on offset six.

Write `R` for that second `ER` and `L` for the final `EL`. Again,
the return from `7:W3` ends at the north arrival supplied by `L`,
so `tau(W4)>=tau(L)+1`. The seven-step path from `W4` to the third
east arrival on offset eight gives `tau(E3)>=tau(L)+8`. Meanwhile
`R -> 5:SL2 -> 8:E4` gives `tau(E4)=tau(R)+2-N`.

Since `R` precedes `L`, these inequalities contradict `E3<E4` in
stable order, exactly as above. The last east arrival on offset six
must therefore turn right. Together with the count theorem, its
east-arrival subsequence is either `ER,EL,ER` or `EL,ER,ER`.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-rigid-five-wake-2026-09-13-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_rigid_five_wake_2026-09-13

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_rigid_five_wake_2026-09-13.md`.

### A wake at offset five forces the three-visit offset-six row

Later result (21 September 2026): [the upper fat-cap theorem](05_Negative_skew.md#source-research-notes-odd-skew-upper-fat122-exclusion-2026-09-21-md) excludes family A. Family B remains open. The local derivation and earlier family status below are retained.

At either `(1,2,2)` cap in the `(1,-3)` drift orbit, suppose inward
offset four is non-wake and offset five is wake. Then the stable word at
offset six is `ER,EL,ER`, in inward heading labels. In particular,

```text
wake offsets:      0,5,6,8,
non-wake offsets:  1,2,3,4,7.
```

The statement uses only zero-displacement returns and holds at either
cap, independently of growth and period. At growth eight it excludes
family C of `odd_skew_fat122_three_visit_wake_families_2026-09-13.md`.
It also shows that the hypotheses of that note exhaust the lower
`(1,2,2)` branch with `omega_4=0`: the lower early-wake theorem supplies
`omega_5=1`, and the present theorem supplies the three-visit row.

#### The possible words at offset six

Use inward offsets, rotating heading labels by 180 degrees at the upper
cap. The previously proved `(1,2,2)` cap normal form is

```text
0: WR
1: NR,SL
2: WR,WL
3: SR,SL,NR,SL
4: WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL
5 A: SR,SL,NR,NL,NR
  B: NR,SL,SR,NL,NR.
```

Put `e=omega_6`, and let `z` be the number of `ER` ports there. Flow
gives the complete count row

```text
(ER,EL,WR,WL)=(z,3-z,5+e-3z,2-z),       z=0,1,2.
```

The south predecessor of `5:SR` has the four-step same-cell return

```text
6:south departure -> 5:SR -> 2:WR -> 3:NR
                  -> second east arrival on 6.
```

Its endpoints are consecutive stable visits. For `z=0` they would
both turn left, so this row is impossible. For `z=2`, nonnegative
counts force `e=1` and the three-visit word `ER,EL,ER`. It remains to
exclude `z=1`.

If the second east arrival is `EL`, its source in this return is
`ER`, necessarily the first east arrival. For word A on offset five
this is the first south departure, so the pair is the initial
`ER,EL`. For word B it is the second south departure, giving the
prefix `WR,WL,ER,EL`.

If the second east arrival is `ER`, its source is `WL`, and the
first east arrival is `EL` before that consecutive pair. This requires
word A: the return step from this `ER` gives a south arrival after
`5:SR`, whereas in word B that `SR` is the last south arrival.

Placing the remaining ports in alternating slots now gives exactly
the following words, each followed by `[WR]^e`:

| Name | Offset five | Offset six |
|---|---|---|
| K1 | A | `ER,EL,WR,EL,WR,WL` |
| K2 | A | `ER,EL,WR,WL,WR,EL` |
| K3 | A | `WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,EL` |
| K4 | B | `WR,WL,ER,EL,WR,EL` |

#### Adjacent west departures

Number the four west departures on offset seven as `W1,...,W4`.
They feed the west arrivals `WR,WL,WL,WR` on offset four. Let `Ni`
denote its north arrivals in stable order. Forced first same-cell
returns give

| Word | Return from W2 | Return from W3 |
|---|---|---|
| K1 | `W2 -> N1` | `W3 -> N3` |
| K2 | `W2 -> N1` | `W3 -> N4` |
| K3 | `W2 -> N2` | `W3 -> N4` |
| K4 | `W2 -> N2` | `W3 -> N4` |

The first path begins

```text
W2 -> 4:first WL -> 3:SR -> 0:WR -> 1:NR -> 4:ER
   -> 3:first SL -> first east arrival on 6.
```

For K3 this east arrival is `EL`, and its north step completes the
return in eight steps. In the other words it is `ER`; the path
continues through `5:SR,2:WR,3:NR` to the second east arrival `EL`,
then north, for a twelve-step return. The second return is always

```text
W3 -> 4:second WL -> 3:last SL -> 6:last EL -> north arrival on 7.
```

It has length four. The optional final `WR` on offset six changes
none of the displayed north ordinals.

Recall the adjacent-west ordinal test. If adjacent west departures
return to `Na,Nb` and the first is `SR`, its consecutive target
`Na=NL` is the second west departure. That departure must return to
the next north arrival, so `b=a+1`. If this equality fails, the first
west departure must instead be `NL=N(a-1)`. In particular, `a=1`
and `b!=2` are incompatible.

This immediately excludes K1 and K2. In K3 and K4 it gives
`W2=NL=N1`. For K4 the resulting eight-step return

```text
6:first WR -> 7:W2 -> 4:first WL -> 3:SR -> 0:WR
           -> 1:NR -> 4:ER -> 3:first SL -> 6:first ER
```

has two right-turn endpoints, with no intermediate offset-six visit.
Thus K4 is also impossible.

#### The remaining word K3

For K3, suppose `W3=NL`. Its consecutive target `N4=NR` would make
it `N3`, fed by the second `WR` on offset six. The four-step return

```text
6:second WR -> 7:W3 -> 4:second WL -> 3:last SL -> 6:last EL
```

would then skip the intervening `WL,ER` in that stable word.
Therefore `W3=SR` and its consecutive target is `N4=NL=W4`.
Since `W2` is the first north arrival, `W1=SR`.

There are `4+e` north arrivals on offset seven. Alternation fixes
the word through `W4` as

```text
7: SR,NL,NR,SL,NR,SL,SR,NL.
```

For `e=0` this is the whole word. For `e=1` it is followed by `NR`
and possibly a final `SL`. In particular, no south arrival can be
the stable successor of `W4=NL`: its successor is either absent or
the remaining `NR`.

Let `E1,E2,E3` be the east arrivals on offset eight, fed by
`5:SL,NR1,NR2`. Two fixed paths are

```text
7:W1=SR -> 4:first WR -> 5:first NR -> E2,
7:W4=NL -> 4:last WR -> 5:NL -> 2:WL -> 1:SL
        -> 4:EL -> 5:last NR -> E3.
```

If `E3=ER`, its south step completes an eight-step return from
`W4` to a south arrival on offset seven. This contradicts the
successor just identified. Hence `E3=EL`.

Now take the offset-eight south departure feeding `W3=SR`. Follow
its south step, the four-step return from `W3` to `W4`, and the
seven-step path from `W4` to `E3`. This is a twelve-step first
same-cell return to offset eight. Its source must be the right-turn
east arrival immediately preceding `E3`, namely `E2=ER`.

But then the first displayed path completes the four-step return

```text
7:W1=SR -> 4:first WR -> 5:first NR -> E2=ER -> 7:W3=SR.
```

Both endpoints turn right. This contradiction excludes K3 and
finishes the exclusion of `z=1`.

#### The forced wake pattern and family C

Only `z=2,e=1` remains, with word `ER,EL,ER`. The zero-displacement
return proof in `odd_skew_fat122_three_visit_six_2026-09-12.md` now
forces offset seven to be non-wake and offset eight to be wake.
None of those steps uses the lower-cap voltage inequality, so the
conclusion holds at either cap.

In family C of the wake-family note, the upper cap is `(1,2,2)`
with inward offsets four non-wake and five wake. The present theorem
therefore forces a wake at upper offset eight, or at level `W-8`.
Yet that level is even in residue two, whose only even wake in C is
eight. This would require `W=16`, whereas C requires `W>=22`.
Thus family C is excluded at every period and width.

Combining the present theorem with the lower early-wake bound gives
the following remaining alternatives whenever the lower cap is
`(1,2,2)` and level four is non-wake:

```text
A: W=6m+2,
   O={0,5,6,8,6r+4,6p+2,W-4,W},
   1<=r<p<=m-1, r+p even, upper cap (1,2,2);

B: W=6m+4,
   O={0,5,6,8,6p+2,6r+4,W-2,W},
   2<=p<r<=m-1, p+r odd, upper cap (1,2,3).
```

These two families remain open. The later lower-band exclusions in
`odd_skew_fat122_four_six_band_exclusion_2026-09-14.md` and
`odd_skew_fat122_adjacent_wake_band_exclusion_2026-09-14.md`, together
with the earlier exclusions, rule out every lower `(1,2,2)` cap
with a wake at level four. Thus A and B now exhaust this lower
cap profile. The other cap profiles in the orbit remain outside
this classification.

#### The common non-wake band at the upper cap

The remaining parameter restrictions give a fixed interval for the next
local argument. Write all wake positions as upper inward offsets.

In family A, put `h=m-p` and `k=m-r`. The exact ordered wake offsets are

```text
0,4,6h,6k-2,6m-6,6m-4,6m-3,6m+2,
1<=h,       h+2<=k<=m-1,       h+k even.
```

Indeed, `r<p` and their common parity give `p-r>=2`, hence `k>=h+2`.
The first wake after offset four is therefore at offset `6h`. If `h=1`,
the next one is at least sixteen, so offsets seven through fifteen are
non-wake. If `h>=2`, offsets five through eleven are non-wake.

In family B, put `h=m-r` and `k=m-p`. Its ordered wake offsets are

```text
0,2,6h,6k+2,6m-4,6m-2,6m-1,6m+4,
1<=h<k<=m-2,       k-h odd.
```

For `h=1`, the wake at offset six is followed by no further wake before
offset fourteen. For `h>=2`, no wake follows offset two before offset
twelve.

Thus both surviving families have upper offsets seven through eleven
non-wake, and upper offset five is also non-wake. Offset six may be wake.
The cap profiles and the wake at offset four in A or offset two in B
distinguish the two local problems. An exclusion of these upper patterns
would complete the lower `(1,2,2)` profile, since the later exclusions
force `omega_4=0`; such an exclusion is not proved here.

The later note `odd_skew_upper_a_single_er_reduction_2026-09-14.md`
uses these non-wake conditions to reduce the upper single-`ER`
branch of A to `h=1` and one fixed offset-seven word. That
last word is excluded in
`odd_skew_upper_a_single_er_exclusion_2026-09-14.md`.
The upper two-`ER` branch of A and family B remain open.

<a id="source-research-notes-odd-skew-fat122-seventh-level-2026-09-13-md"></a>

## odd_skew_fat122_seventh_level_2026-09-13

Source: `research-notes/odd_skew_fat122_seventh_level_2026-09-13.md`.

### Level-seven order when the lower fourth level is wake

Consider a lower `(1,2,2)` cap in the `(1,-3)` orbit, with
`omega_4=1`. Write `c=omega_5`, `e=omega_6`, and `f=omega_7`.
The lower-band theorem fixes the level-five word starting `SR,SL`
and the initial level-six triple `ER,EL,ER`.

Put `k=1-c+e`, the number of `WR` ports on level six. Level seven
has the following necessary word for some `0<=j<=k`:

```text
SR,SL,SR,NL,SR,SL,(NR,SL)^j,SR,(SL,NR)^(k-j),[SL]^(1-f).
```

Its west departures, in order, are always `SR,SR,NL,SR,SR`.
When `(c,e)=(0,1)`, the endpoint `j=2` is impossible.

There is a further restriction in the surviving band `(c,e)=(1,1)`.
If `j=1`, then `f=0`, level eight is wake, and it has one of the
two words

```text
ER,EL,WR,WL,WR,WL,WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL,ER;
WR,EL,WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,WL,ER,WL,WR,EL,WR,WL,ER.
```

This subcase therefore has exactly the wakes `0,4,5,6,8` through
level eight. Its continuation is not excluded. All statements here
are all-period lower-cap restrictions.

#### The last two west departures

Use the common rows from
